## Interpretation and scoring

The current thematic score is 0.55 × IDF-weighted shared-topic Jaccard + 0.15 × co-occurrence bridges + 0.15 × independent abstract similarity + 0.05 × intermediate topic path + 0.10 × independent full-text similarity. Shared extracted topics receive most of the weight; indirect paths and language similarity have less influence. A thematic candidate must have at least one shared extracted topic, even if other components produce a positive score. All pair calculations remain in the snapshot for audit; pairs without shared topics are omitted from thematic arcs, the app's candidate list and this report's candidate section. Missing components contribute zero; weights are not renormalised per pair. Co-occurrence bridges and abstract similarity use the mean of their strongest three comparisons; the intermediate component uses the strongest two-hop concept path. TF-IDF uses unigrams and bigrams with smoothed IDF and L2 normalisation. Full-text similarity uses the mean of the strongest three independent body-text comparisons, with a separate TF-IDF corpus from abstracts. Joint papers are excluded from both language comparisons. These choices are transparent exploratory heuristics, not calibrated probabilities or mathematical proofs. Vocabulary entries normalise terminology; no mathematical relationships are seeded. Co-occurrence alone only creates candidate RELATED_TO edges. No GENERALISES, SPECIAL_CASE_OF or USES_METHOD edges are inferred without explicit reviewed evidence. Coauthorship and shared collaborators are reported separately from the thematic score. Shared broad concepts may inflate rankings; small samples, missing abstracts and provider author-cluster errors limit recall and precision.

## Review and evaluation

Use the application's Review tab or `python -m kgmap.cli export-reviews`, edit the JSON, then `import-reviews`. Review IDs persist across refreshes. Rejected extraction and concept-association edges are removed before visible scores are recomputed. Accepting a candidate records a mathematician's opinion; it does not turn similarity into a proven theorem. Corrected decisions require a note and preserve the original evidence. A scientific evaluation still needs a mathematician-labelled set: check author-cluster precision, topic extraction precision/recall, and connection usefulness, then compare against a shared-keywords-only baseline. No accuracy claim is made from software tests.
